If I sit down to a full meal, it feels like I’ve kind of blown the whole day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking a lot about your weight or body shape, or feel that they are very important to how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m obsessed or anything, but before a meeting, or like if I’m going on a date, I definitely check the mirror. Mostly my stomach and my face. If my face looks kind of puffy or my shirt pulls weird, I start thinking, great, now I look soft.\n\nAnd in sales, people size you up fast—like, before you even say anything. I had this old manager who used to say your appearance walks into the room before you do, which, I don’t know, stuck with me. So if I think I look sloppy, I feel like people are going to see me as weak or not put together. So yeah, I guess it matters more than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything on purpose to try to keep from gaining weight, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything extreme like that. The throwing up thing, no, that’s not really… I mean, I hate throwing up, even when I had food poisoning once after this client dinner—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI do try to stay on top of things, like not let a meal get out of hand. And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? Sales is weird because you’re either sitting for hours or you’re pacing around a showroom pretending you’re not checking your phone every two seconds.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m doing “weight loss tricks” or whatever. I just try not to overdo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, fast, or avoid eating certain types of food because you're worried about gaining weight or because you're trying to stay in control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do skip meals, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like “fasting” exactly. Most days it’s coffee in the morning, then if I’m between sales calls maybe I’ll have a protein bar, just something quick. Lunch is hard anyway because I’m usually driving or meeting someone, and honestly those client lunches can turn into this whole thing where everyone orders fries and appetizers and then you’re stuck sitting there pretending you’re relaxed.\n\nThen at night I’ll keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I mean, not like a written rulebook or anything, but if I have, like, a client dinner where everyone’s ordering drinks and sides and dessert—especially if I’m in Miami for the weekend or something, because there’s always some big meal—I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even up a pound, I kind of panic.

It’s not rational, I know a pound can be, like, water or salt or whatever, but in the moment it feels like, okay, I’m losing control, I let it go too far. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.

Lunch is hard anyway because I’m usually driving or meeting someone, and honestly those client lunches can turn into this whole thing where everyone orders fries and appetizers and then you’re stuck sitting there pretending you’re relaxed.\n\nThen at night I’ll keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see yourself differently than others might describe you, or do you feel like certain parts of your body are bigger than they really are?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, kind of. Like before a meeting or if I’m getting ready for a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture, I guess. I go straight to my stomach, or my face.\n\nIf my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird, then I’m like, okay, I look soft. And then it turns into this whole thing in my head, like people are going to read that as sloppy or weak or like I don’t have my life together. Which is stupid, maybe, because most people are probably just thinking about themselves, but in sales you get used to watching every little reaction from people, you know? So it’s hard not to think they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever push yourself to exercise more, eat much less, or make any special rules around eating if you feel like you've eaten too much or had something 'unhealthy'?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like a written rulebook or anything, but if I have, like, a client dinner where everyone’s ordering drinks and sides and dessert—especially if I’m in Miami for the weekend or something, because there’s always some big meal—I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even up a pound, I kind of panic.\n\nIt’s not rational, I know a pound can be, like, water or salt or whatever, but in the moment it feels like, okay, I’m losing control, I let it go too far. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever make yourself exercise more or eat a lot less the day after you’ve had something you consider unhealthy, like a dessert or a heavier meal?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t love admitting that, but it does feel tied together.

Like before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not really thinking, “Do I look fine overall?” I go straight to my stomach or my face. And if my face looks puffy or my shirt makes my stomach look… soft, then it’s like my whole confidence drops. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I’m not on top of things.

Which is probably dramatic, I know. I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.

I mean, from my side, I don’t really see it that way—my suits just fit sharper now, which in sales kind of matters, you know? People notice presentation. But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and they’ve been making a big deal out of it, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite or are you eating less than before?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m eating less, yeah. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then maybe I’ll grab a protein bar between sales calls if I need something. It’s hard when you’re driving all over and meeting clients—honestly half the time lunch is just this awkward thing people use to waste an hour.\n\nAt night I’ll usually keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. If I sit down to a full meal, it feels like I’ve kind of blown the whole day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking a lot about your weight or body shape, or feel that they are very important to how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m obsessed or anything, but before a meeting, or like if I’m going on a date, I definitely check the mirror. Mostly my stomach and my face. If my face looks kind of puffy or my shirt pulls weird, I start thinking, great, now I look soft.\n\nAnd in sales, people size you up fast—like, before you even say anything. I had this old manager who used to say your appearance walks into the room before you do, which, I don’t know, stuck with me. So if I think I look sloppy, I feel like people are going to see me as weak or not put together. So yeah, I guess it matters more than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything on purpose to try to keep from gaining weight, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything extreme like that. The throwing up thing, no, that’s not really… I mean, I hate throwing up, even when I had food poisoning once after this client dinner—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI do try to stay on top of things, like not let a meal get out of hand. And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? Sales is weird because you’re either sitting for hours or you’re pacing around a showroom pretending you’re not checking your phone every two seconds.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m doing “weight loss tricks” or whatever. I just try not to overdo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, fast, or avoid eating certain types of food because you're worried about gaining weight or because you're trying to stay in control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do skip meals, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like “fasting” exactly. Lunch is hard anyway because I’m usually driving or meeting someone, and honestly those client lunches can turn into this whole thing where everyone orders fries and appetizers and then you’re stuck sitting there pretending you’re relaxed.\n\nThen at night I’ll keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see yourself differently than others might describe you, or do you feel like certain parts of your body are bigger than they really are?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, kind of. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself exercise more or eat a lot less the day after you’ve had something you consider unhealthy, like a dessert or a heavier meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. Like if I have a client dinner and it’s heavier than I wanted, or if I’m out in Miami for a weekend and there’s just food everywhere, I’ll check the scale after. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I’m not on top of things.\n\nWhich is probably dramatic, I know. I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that your weight or body shape has an undue influence on how you judge yourself overall?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever push yourself to exercise more, eat much less, or make any special rules around eating if you feel like you've eaten too much or had something 'unhealthy'?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like a written rulebook or anything, but if I have, like, a client dinner where everyone’s ordering drinks and sides and dessert—especially if I’m in Miami for the weekend or something, because there’s always some big meal—I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even up a pound, I kind of panic.\n\nIt’s not rational, I know a pound can be, like, water or salt or whatever, but in the moment it feels like, okay, I’m losing control, I let it go too far. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I guess “undue” sounds like a lot, but yeah, it probably does.

Like before I walk into a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft or puffy or whatever, it’s like I immediately feel less… capable, I guess. Like people are going to look at me and think I’m sloppy or weak, even if that doesn’t really make sense.

And I know most people aren’t thinking about that—like they’re probably thinking about their own stuff—but once I notice it, it kind of takes over how I feel about myself.

I mean, from my side, I don’t really see it that way—my suits just fit sharper now, which in sales kind of matters, you know? People notice presentation. But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and they’ve been making a big deal out of it, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite or are you eating less than before?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m eating less, yeah. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then maybe I’ll grab a protein bar between sales calls if I need something. It’s hard when you’re driving all over and meeting clients—honestly half the time lunch is just this awkward thing people use to waste an hour.\n\nAt night I’ll usually keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. If I sit down to a full meal, it feels like I’ve kind of blown the whole day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking a lot about your weight or body shape, or feel that they are very important to how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m obsessed or anything, but before a meeting, or like if I’m going on a date, I definitely check the mirror. Mostly my stomach and my face. If my face looks kind of puffy or my shirt pulls weird, I start thinking, great, now I look soft.\n\nAnd in sales, people size you up fast—like, before you even say anything. I had this old manager who used to say your appearance walks into the room before you do, which, I don’t know, stuck with me. So if I think I look sloppy, I feel like people are going to see me as weak or not put together. So yeah, I guess it matters more than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything on purpose to try to keep from gaining weight, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything extreme like that. The throwing up thing, no, that’s not really… I mean, I hate throwing up, even when I had food poisoning once after this client dinner—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI do try to stay on top of things, like not let a meal get out of hand. And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? Sales is weird because you’re either sitting for hours or you’re pacing around a showroom pretending you’re not checking your phone every two seconds.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m doing “weight loss tricks” or whatever. I just try not to overdo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, fast, or avoid eating certain types of food because you're worried about gaining weight or because you're trying to stay in control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do skip meals, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like “fasting” exactly. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see yourself differently than others might describe you, or do you feel like certain parts of your body are bigger than they really are?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, kind of. Like before a meeting or if I’m getting ready for a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture, I guess. I go straight to my stomach, or my face.\n\nIf my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird, then I’m like, okay, I look soft. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself exercise more or eat a lot less the day after you’ve had something you consider unhealthy, like a dessert or a heavier meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. I don’t love admitting that, but it does feel tied together.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not really thinking, “Do I look fine overall?” I go straight to my stomach or my face. And if my face looks puffy or my shirt makes my stomach look… soft, then it’s like my whole confidence drops. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I’m not on top of things.\n\nWhich is probably dramatic, I know. I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your weight or body shape has an undue influence on how you judge yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess “undue” sounds like a lot, but yeah, it probably does.\n\nLike before I walk into a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft or puffy or whatever, it’s like I immediately feel less… capable, I guess. Like people are going to look at me and think I’m sloppy or weak, even if that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd I know most people aren’t thinking about that—like they’re probably thinking about their own stuff—but once I notice it, it kind of takes over how I feel about myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you look at yourself and feel like you look soft or puffy, have others ever told you that you look different than you perceive yourself to be?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sometimes. Like, someone might say, “You look fine,” or not notice anything at all, and I’m still stuck on it.

It’s usually before a meeting or a date—I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole me, I just zero in on my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird and I think my stomach looks soft, then I start assuming that’s what everyone else is going to see too. Like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not put together.

And I know that’s probably not what they’re thinking. I mean, half the time people are worried about their own presentation or traffic or whatever. But in the moment it feels really obvious to me.

And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? I’m not sure I’d describe it like I can’t stop once I start. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.00, F50.01, F50.02\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister keep saying I’ve gotten too thin. I mean, from my side, I don’t really see it that way—my suits just fit sharper now, which in sales kind of matters, you know? People notice presentation. But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and they’ve been making a big deal out of it, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite or are you eating less than before?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m eating less, yeah. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then maybe I’ll grab a protein bar between sales calls if I need something. It’s hard when you’re driving all over and meeting clients—honestly half the time lunch is just this awkward thing people use to waste an hour.\n\nAt night I’ll usually keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. If I sit down to a full meal, it feels like I’ve kind of blown the whole day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking a lot about your weight or body shape, or feel that they are very important to how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m obsessed or anything, but before a meeting, or like if I’m going on a date, I definitely check the mirror. Mostly my stomach and my face. If my face looks kind of puffy or my shirt pulls weird, I start thinking, great, now I look soft.\n\nAnd in sales, people size you up fast—like, before you even say anything. I had this old manager who used to say your appearance walks into the room before you do, which, I don’t know, stuck with me. So if I think I look sloppy, I feel like people are going to see me as weak or not put together. So yeah, I guess it matters more than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything on purpose to try to keep from gaining weight, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything extreme like that. The throwing up thing, no, that’s not really… I mean, I hate throwing up, even when I had food poisoning once after this client dinner—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI do try to stay on top of things, like not let a meal get out of hand. And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? Sales is weird because you’re either sitting for hours or you’re pacing around a showroom pretending you’re not checking your phone every two seconds.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m doing “weight loss tricks” or whatever. I just try not to overdo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, fast, or avoid eating certain types of food because you're worried about gaining weight or because you're trying to stay in control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do skip meals, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like “fasting” exactly. Most days it’s coffee in the morning, then if I’m between sales calls maybe I’ll have a protein bar, just something quick. Lunch is hard anyway because I’m usually driving or meeting someone, and honestly those client lunches can turn into this whole thing where everyone orders fries and appetizers and then you’re stuck sitting there pretending you’re relaxed.\n\nThen at night I’ll keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see yourself differently than others might describe you, or do you feel like certain parts of your body are bigger than they really are?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, kind of. Like before a meeting or if I’m getting ready for a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture, I guess. I go straight to my stomach, or my face.\n\nIf my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird, then I’m like, okay, I look soft. And then it turns into this whole thing in my head, like people are going to read that as sloppy or weak or like I don’t have my life together. Which is stupid, maybe, because most people are probably just thinking about themselves, but in sales you get used to watching every little reaction from people, you know? So it’s hard not to think they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever push yourself to exercise more, eat much less, or make any special rules around eating if you feel like you've eaten too much or had something 'unhealthy'?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like a written rulebook or anything, but if I have, like, a client dinner where everyone’s ordering drinks and sides and dessert—especially if I’m in Miami for the weekend or something, because there’s always some big meal—I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even up a pound, I kind of panic.\n\nIt’s not rational, I know a pound can be, like, water or salt or whatever, but in the moment it feels like, okay, I’m losing control, I let it go too far. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself exercise more or eat a lot less the day after you’ve had something you consider unhealthy, like a dessert or a heavier meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. Like if I have a client dinner and it’s heavier than I wanted, or if I’m out in Miami for a weekend and there’s just food everywhere, I’ll check the scale after. And if it’s even up, like, one pound, I get this… panic feeling, like I screwed up or I’m losing control.\n\nThen I start planning the next day in my head—what I can cut back, how to keep it smaller. Sometimes I’m not even paying attention to other things because I’m already trying to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of how you’re doing in life, or how you feel about yourself, is strongly connected to your weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love admitting that, but it does feel tied together.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not really thinking, “Do I look fine overall?” I go straight to my stomach or my face. And if my face looks puffy or my shirt makes my stomach look… soft, then it’s like my whole confidence drops. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I’m not on top of things.\n\nWhich is probably dramatic, I know. I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your weight or body shape has an undue influence on how you judge yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess “undue” sounds like a lot, but yeah, it probably does.\n\nLike before I walk into a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft or puffy or whatever, it’s like I immediately feel less… capable, I guess. Like people are going to look at me and think I’m sloppy or weak, even if that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd I know most people aren’t thinking about that—like they’re probably thinking about their own stuff—but once I notice it, it kind of takes over how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself and feel like you look soft or puffy, have others ever told you that you look different than you perceive yourself to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Like, someone might say, “You look fine,” or not notice anything at all, and I’m still stuck on it.\n\nIt’s usually before a meeting or a date—I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole me, I just zero in on my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird and I think my stomach looks soft, then I start assuming that’s what everyone else is going to see too. Like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not put together.\n\nAnd I know that’s probably not what they’re thinking. I mean, half the time people are worried about their own presentation or traffic or whatever. But in the moment it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating, like you can't stop eating once you start, and then do something to try to make up for it, like eating a lot less or exercising more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it like I can’t stop once I start. I mean… sometimes I eat more than I planned, like anyone does, especially if I’m stressed or there’s food around at work—people bring in donuts or whatever, and then everyone’s standing around talking about numbers and orders and I’m thinking about whether I should’ve had one.\n\nBut the “lost control” thing… I don’t know. I go straight to my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft or puffy or whatever, it’s like I immediately feel less… capable, I guess. Like people are going to look at me and think I’m sloppy or weak, even if that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd I know most people aren’t thinking about that—like they’re probably thinking about their own stuff—but once I notice it, it kind of takes over how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself and feel like you look soft or puffy, have others ever told you that you look different than you perceive yourself to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Like, someone might say, “You look fine,” or not notice anything at all, and I’m still stuck on it.\n\nIt’s usually before a meeting or a date—I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole me, I just zero in on my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird and I think my stomach looks soft, then I start assuming that’s what everyone else is going to see too. Like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not put together.\n\nAnd I know that’s probably not what they’re thinking. I mean, half the time people are worried about their own presentation or traffic or whatever. But in the moment it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating, like you can't stop eating once you start, and then do something to try to make up for it, like eating a lot less or exercising more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it like I can’t stop once I start. I mean… sometimes I eat more than I planned, like anyone does, especially if I’m stressed or there’s food around at work—people bring in donuts or whatever, and then everyone’s standing around talking about numbers and orders and I’m thinking about whether I should’ve had one.\n\nBut the “lost control” thing… I don’t know. That sounds a little different than what I usually think of. I do get in my head about it afterward, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by making up for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, from my side, I don’t really see it that way—my suits just fit sharper now, which in sales kind of matters, you know? People notice presentation. But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and they’ve been making a big deal out of it, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite or are you eating less than before?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m eating less, yeah. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then maybe I’ll grab a protein bar between sales calls if I need something. It’s hard when you’re driving all over and meeting clients—honestly half the time lunch is just this awkward thing people use to waste an hour.\n\nAt night I’ll usually keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. If I sit down to a full meal, it feels like I’ve kind of blown the whole day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking a lot about your weight or body shape, or feel that they are very important to how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m obsessed or anything, but before a meeting, or like if I’m going on a date, I definitely check the mirror. Mostly my stomach and my face. If my face looks kind of puffy or my shirt pulls weird, I start thinking, great, now I look soft.\n\nAnd in sales, people size you up fast—like, before you even say anything. I had this old manager who used to say your appearance walks into the room before you do, which, I don’t know, stuck with me. So if I think I look sloppy, I feel like people are going to see me as weak or not put together. So yeah, I guess it matters more than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything on purpose to try to keep from gaining weight, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything extreme like that. The throwing up thing, no, that’s not really… I mean, I hate throwing up, even when I had food poisoning once after this client dinner—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI do try to stay on top of things, like not let a meal get out of hand. And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? Sales is weird because you’re either sitting for hours or you’re pacing around a showroom pretending you’re not checking your phone every two seconds.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m doing “weight loss tricks” or whatever. I just try not to overdo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, fast, or avoid eating certain types of food because you're worried about gaining weight or because you're trying to stay in control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do skip meals, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like “fasting” exactly. Most days it’s coffee in the morning, then if I’m between sales calls maybe I’ll have a protein bar, just something quick. Lunch is hard anyway because I’m usually driving or meeting someone, and honestly those client lunches can turn into this whole thing where everyone orders fries and appetizers and then you’re stuck sitting there pretending you’re relaxed.\n\nThen at night I’ll keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see yourself differently than others might describe you, or do you feel like certain parts of your body are bigger than they really are?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, kind of. Like before a meeting or if I’m getting ready for a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture, I guess. I go straight to my stomach, or my face.\n\nIf my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird, then I’m like, okay, I look soft. And then it turns into this whole thing in my head, like people are going to read that as sloppy or weak or like I don’t have my life together. Which is stupid, maybe, because most people are probably just thinking about themselves, but in sales you get used to watching every little reaction from people, you know? So it’s hard not to think they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever push yourself to exercise more, eat much less, or make any special rules around eating if you feel like you've eaten too much or had something 'unhealthy'?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like a written rulebook or anything, but if I have, like, a client dinner where everyone’s ordering drinks and sides and dessert—especially if I’m in Miami for the weekend or something, because there’s always some big meal—I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even up a pound, I kind of panic.\n\nIt’s not rational, I know a pound can be, like, water or salt or whatever, but in the moment it feels like, okay, I’m losing control, I let it go too far. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself exercise more or eat a lot less the day after you’ve had something you consider unhealthy, like a dessert or a heavier meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. Like if I have a client dinner and it’s heavier than I wanted, or if I’m out in Miami for a weekend and there’s just food everywhere, I’ll check the scale after. And if it’s even up, like, one pound, I get this… panic feeling, like I screwed up or I’m losing control.\n\nThen I start planning the next day in my head—what I can cut back, how to keep it smaller. Sometimes I’m not even paying attention to other things because I’m already trying to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of how you’re doing in life, or how you feel about yourself, is strongly connected to your weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love admitting that, but it does feel tied together.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not really thinking, “Do I look fine overall?” I go straight to my stomach or my face. And if my face looks puffy or my shirt makes my stomach look… soft, then it’s like my whole confidence drops. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I’m not on top of things.\n\nWhich is probably dramatic, I know. I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your weight or body shape has an undue influence on how you judge yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess “undue” sounds like a lot, but yeah, it probably does.\n\nLike before I walk into a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. So yeah, I guess it matters more than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything on purpose to try to keep from gaining weight, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything extreme like that. The throwing up thing, no, that’s not really… I mean, I hate throwing up, even when I had food poisoning once after this client dinner—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI do try to stay on top of things, like not let a meal get out of hand. And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? Sales is weird because you’re either sitting for hours or you’re pacing around a showroom pretending you’re not checking your phone every two seconds.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m doing “weight loss tricks” or whatever. I just try not to overdo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, fast, or avoid eating certain types of food because you're worried about gaining weight or because you're trying to stay in control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do skip meals, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like “fasting” exactly. Most days it’s coffee in the morning, then if I’m between sales calls maybe I’ll have a protein bar, just something quick. Lunch is hard anyway because I’m usually driving or meeting someone, and honestly those client lunches can turn into this whole thing where everyone orders fries and appetizers and then you’re stuck sitting there pretending you’re relaxed.\n\nThen at night I’ll keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see yourself differently than others might describe you, or do you feel like certain parts of your body are bigger than they really are?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, kind of. Like before a meeting or if I’m getting ready for a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture, I guess. I go straight to my stomach, or my face.\n\nIf my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird, then I’m like, okay, I look soft. And then it turns into this whole thing in my head, like people are going to read that as sloppy or weak or like I don’t have my life together. Which is stupid, maybe, because most people are probably just thinking about themselves, but in sales you get used to watching every little reaction from people, you know? So it’s hard not to think they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever push yourself to exercise more, eat much less, or make any special rules around eating if you feel like you've eaten too much or had something 'unhealthy'?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like a written rulebook or anything, but if I have, like, a client dinner where everyone’s ordering drinks and sides and dessert—especially if I’m in Miami for the weekend or something, because there’s always some big meal—I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even up a pound, I kind of panic.\n\nIt’s not rational, I know a pound can be, like, water or salt or whatever, but in the moment it feels like, okay, I’m losing control, I let it go too far. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself exercise more or eat a lot less the day after you’ve had something you consider unhealthy, like a dessert or a heavier meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. Like if I have a client dinner and it’s heavier than I wanted, or if I’m out in Miami for a weekend and there’s just food everywhere, I’ll check the scale after. And if it’s even up, like, one pound, I get this… panic feeling, like I screwed up or I’m losing control.\n\nThen I start planning the next day in my head—what I can cut back, how to keep it smaller. Sometimes I’m not even paying attention to other things because I’m already trying to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of how you’re doing in life, or how you feel about yourself, is strongly connected to your weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love admitting that, but it does feel tied together.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not really thinking, “Do I look fine overall?” I go straight to my stomach or my face. And if my face looks puffy or my shirt makes my stomach look… soft, then it’s like my whole confidence drops. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I’m not on top of things.\n\nWhich is probably dramatic, I know. I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your weight or body shape has an undue influence on how you judge yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess “undue” sounds like a lot, but yeah, it probably does.\n\nLike before I walk into a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft or puffy or whatever, it’s like I immediately feel less… capable, I guess. Like people are going to look at me and think I’m sloppy or weak, even if that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd I know most people aren’t thinking about that—like they’re probably thinking about their own stuff—but once I notice it, it kind of takes over how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself and feel like you look soft or puffy, have others ever told you that you look different than you perceive yourself to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Like, someone might say, “You look fine,” or not notice anything at all, and I’m still stuck on it.\n\nIt’s usually before a meeting or a date—I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole me, I just zero in on my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird and I think my stomach looks soft, then I start assuming that’s what everyone else is going to see too. Like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not put together.\n\nAnd I know that’s probably not what they’re thinking. I mean, half the time people are worried about their own presentation or traffic or whatever. But in the moment it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating, like you can't stop eating once you start, and then do something to try to make up for it, like eating a lot less or exercising more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it like I can’t stop once I start. I mean… sometimes I eat more than I planned, like anyone does, especially if I’m stressed or there’s food around at work—people bring in donuts or whatever, and then everyone’s standing around talking about numbers and orders and I’m thinking about whether I should’ve had one.\n\nBut the “lost control” thing… I don’t know. That sounds a little different than what I usually think of. I do get in my head about it afterward, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by making up for it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you couldn’t stop eating or control what or how much you were eating, even if you didn’t want to eat that much?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, “couldn’t stop” feels… strong. There are times I eat more than I meant to, like if I’m at work and someone brings in food and I’m distracted talking to people, and then afterward I’m like, why did I do that?\n\nBut I don’t know if it’s that I couldn’t control it, exactly. Sometimes it’s more like I’m thinking about it so much that the whole thing gets weird. Like I’ll have one thing and then spend the next hour replaying it, which is probably not what you’re asking. I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is between eating more than planned and “losing control.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t know if I’d call it a very large amount. Like, I hear that and I think of someone eating, I don’t know, a whole fridge worth of food or something, and that’s not really how it feels for me.

Sorry, I’m kind of circling around it. I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, clear episodes where I’m eating a huge amount really fast and completely out of control, if that’s what you mean.

I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your weight or body shape has an undue influence on how you judge yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess “undue” sounds like a lot, but yeah, it probably does.\n\nLike before I walk into a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft or puffy or whatever, it’s like I immediately feel less… capable, I guess. Like people are going to look at me and think I’m sloppy or weak, even if that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd I know most people aren’t thinking about that—like they’re probably thinking about their own stuff—but once I notice it, it kind of takes over how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself and feel like you look soft or puffy, have others ever told you that you look different than you perceive yourself to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Like, someone might say, “You look fine,” or not notice anything at all, and I’m still stuck on it.\n\nIt’s usually before a meeting or a date—I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole me, I just zero in on my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird and I think my stomach looks soft, then I start assuming that’s what everyone else is going to see too. Like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not put together.\n\nAnd I know that’s probably not what they’re thinking. I mean, half the time people are worried about their own presentation or traffic or whatever. But in the moment it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating, like you can't stop eating once you start, and then do something to try to make up for it, like eating a lot less or exercising more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it like I can’t stop once I start. I mean… sometimes I eat more than I planned, like anyone does, especially if I’m stressed or there’s food around at work—people bring in donuts or whatever, and then everyone’s standing around talking about numbers and orders and I’m thinking about whether I should’ve had one.\n\nBut the “lost control” thing… I don’t know. That sounds a little different than what I usually think of. I do get in my head about it afterward, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by making up for it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you couldn’t stop eating or control what or how much you were eating, even if you didn’t want to eat that much?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, “couldn’t stop” feels… strong. There are times I eat more than I meant to, like if I’m at work and someone brings in food and I’m distracted talking to people, and then afterward I’m like, why did I do that?\n\nBut I don’t know if it’s that I couldn’t control it, exactly. Sometimes it’s more like I’m thinking about it so much that the whole thing gets weird. Like I’ll have one thing and then spend the next hour replaying it, which is probably not what you’re asking. I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is between eating more than planned and “losing control.”\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you were eating a very large amount of food in a short time and felt like you couldn’t control how much you were eating or stop yourself from eating once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a very large amount. Like, I hear that and I think of someone eating, I don’t know, a whole fridge worth of food or something, and that’s not really how it feels for me.\n\nThere are times where I’ll eat more than I meant to and then get really uncomfortable thinking about it, but the “couldn’t stop myself” part is harder to say yes to. I usually feel like I’m aware of it the whole time, maybe too aware. Like I’m watching myself and judging it while it’s happening.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling around it. I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, clear episodes where I’m eating a huge amount really fast and completely out of control, if that’s what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean… I know you’re asking it pretty specifically, and I don’t want to dodge it. I just don’t think I have a clean example of that.

Like, “very large amount in a short time” makes me think of something obvious, and for me it’s more… I’ll eat something I wasn’t planning on, and then I’m stuck thinking about it way too much. But I wouldn’t say I’m just gone and can’t stop myself. I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing.

Sorry, I keep getting caught on the wording. It’s kind of like at work when a client asks a question that sounds simple, but depending on how they define “bulk order,” the answer changes. Not that this is the same thing, obviously. I guess I’m saying I’m not sure I’ve had what you’re describing.

But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and they’ve been making a big deal out of it, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite or are you eating less than before?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m eating less, yeah. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then maybe I’ll grab a protein bar between sales calls if I need something. If I sit down to a full meal, it feels like I’ve kind of blown the whole day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking a lot about your weight or body shape, or feel that they are very important to how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m obsessed or anything, but before a meeting, or like if I’m going on a date, I definitely check the mirror. Mostly my stomach and my face. And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? Sales is weird because you’re either sitting for hours or you’re pacing around a showroom pretending you’re not checking your phone every two seconds.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m doing “weight loss tricks” or whatever. I just try not to overdo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, fast, or avoid eating certain types of food because you're worried about gaining weight or because you're trying to stay in control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do skip meals, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like “fasting” exactly. Most days it’s coffee in the morning, then if I’m between sales calls maybe I’ll have a protein bar, just something quick. Lunch is hard anyway because I’m usually driving or meeting someone, and honestly those client lunches can turn into this whole thing where everyone orders fries and appetizers and then you’re stuck sitting there pretending you’re relaxed.\n\nThen at night I’ll keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see yourself differently than others might describe you, or do you feel like certain parts of your body are bigger than they really are?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, kind of. Like before a meeting or if I’m getting ready for a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture, I guess. I go straight to my stomach, or my face.\n\nIf my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird, then I’m like, okay, I look soft. And then it turns into this whole thing in my head, like people are going to read that as sloppy or weak or like I don’t have my life together. Which is stupid, maybe, because most people are probably just thinking about themselves, but in sales you get used to watching every little reaction from people, you know? So it’s hard not to think they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever push yourself to exercise more, eat much less, or make any special rules around eating if you feel like you've eaten too much or had something 'unhealthy'?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like a written rulebook or anything, but if I have, like, a client dinner where everyone’s ordering drinks and sides and dessert—especially if I’m in Miami for the weekend or something, because there’s always some big meal—I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even up a pound, I kind of panic.\n\nIt’s not rational, I know a pound can be, like, water or salt or whatever, but in the moment it feels like, okay, I’m losing control, I let it go too far. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself exercise more or eat a lot less the day after you’ve had something you consider unhealthy, like a dessert or a heavier meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. Like if I have a client dinner and it’s heavier than I wanted, or if I’m out in Miami for a weekend and there’s just food everywhere, I’ll check the scale after. And if it’s even up, like, one pound, I get this… panic feeling, like I screwed up or I’m losing control.\n\nThen I start planning the next day in my head—what I can cut back, how to keep it smaller. Sometimes I’m not even paying attention to other things because I’m already trying to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of how you’re doing in life, or how you feel about yourself, is strongly connected to your weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love admitting that, but it does feel tied together.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not really thinking, “Do I look fine overall?” I go straight to my stomach or my face. And if my face looks puffy or my shirt makes my stomach look… soft, then it’s like my whole confidence drops. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I’m not on top of things.\n\nWhich is probably dramatic, I know. I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your weight or body shape has an undue influence on how you judge yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess “undue” sounds like a lot, but yeah, it probably does.\n\nLike before I walk into a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft or puffy or whatever, it’s like I immediately feel less… capable, I guess. Like people are going to look at me and think I’m sloppy or weak, even if that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd I know most people aren’t thinking about that—like they’re probably thinking about their own stuff—but once I notice it, it kind of takes over how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself and feel like you look soft or puffy, have others ever told you that you look different than you perceive yourself to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Like, someone might say, “You look fine,” or not notice anything at all, and I’m still stuck on it.\n\nIt’s usually before a meeting or a date—I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole me, I just zero in on my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird and I think my stomach looks soft, then I start assuming that’s what everyone else is going to see too. Like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not put together.\n\nAnd I know that’s probably not what they’re thinking. I mean, half the time people are worried about their own presentation or traffic or whatever. But in the moment it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating, like you can't stop eating once you start, and then do something to try to make up for it, like eating a lot less or exercising more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it like I can’t stop once I start. I mean… sometimes I eat more than I planned, like anyone does, especially if I’m stressed or there’s food around at work—people bring in donuts or whatever, and then everyone’s standing around talking about numbers and orders and I’m thinking about whether I should’ve had one.\n\nBut the “lost control” thing… I don’t know. and dates unless I can control the menu, so I’ve been turning down invitations and keeping more to myself.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / sales_representative_wholesale_or_manufacturing / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S044\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you couldn’t stop eating or control what or how much you were eating, even if you didn’t want to eat that much?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, “couldn’t stop” feels… strong. There are times I eat more than I meant to, like if I’m at work and someone brings in food and I’m distracted talking to people, and then afterward I’m like, why did I do that?\n\nBut I don’t know if it’s that I couldn’t control it, exactly. Sometimes it’s more like I’m thinking about it so much that the whole thing gets weird. Like I’ll have one thing and then spend the next hour replaying it, which is probably not what you’re asking. I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is between eating more than planned and “losing control.”\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you were eating a very large amount of food in a short time and felt like you couldn’t control how much you were eating or stop yourself from eating once you started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a very large amount. Like, I hear that and I think of someone eating, I don’t know, a whole fridge worth of food or something, and that’s not really how it feels for me.\n\nThere are times where I’ll eat more than I meant to and then get really uncomfortable thinking about it, but the “couldn’t stop myself” part is harder to say yes to. I usually feel like I’m aware of it the whole time, maybe too aware. Like I’m watching myself and judging it while it’s happening.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling around it. I guess I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve had, like, clear episodes where I’m eating a huge amount really fast and completely out of control, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you were eating a very large amount of food in a short time and felt like you couldn’t control how much you were eating or stop yourself from eating once you started?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking it pretty specifically, and I don’t want to dodge it. I just don’t think I have a clean example of that.\n\nLike, “very large amount in a short time” makes me think of something obvious, and for me it’s more… I’ll eat something I wasn’t planning on, and then I’m stuck thinking about it way too much. But I wouldn’t say I’m just gone and can’t stop myself. I’m usually very aware of what I’m doing.\n\nSorry, I keep getting caught on the wording. It’s kind of like at work when a client asks a question that sounds simple, but depending on how they define “bulk order,” the answer changes. Not that this is the same thing, obviously. I guess I’m saying I’m not sure I’ve had what you’re describing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had episodes where you ate a definitely larger amount of food than most people would in a short time, like a couple of hours, and felt like you couldn’t control your eating during that time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m mostly here because HR at work and my sister keep saying I’ve gotten too thin. I mean, from my side, I don’t really see it that way—my suits just fit sharper now, which in sales kind of matters, you know? People notice presentation. But, yeah, my weight has kept dropping since the divorce, and they’ve been making a big deal out of it, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your appetite or are you eating less than before?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m eating less, yeah. Most mornings it’s just coffee, then maybe I’ll grab a protein bar between sales calls if I need something. It’s hard when you’re driving all over and meeting clients—honestly half the time lunch is just this awkward thing people use to waste an hour.\n\nAt night I’ll usually keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. If I sit down to a full meal, it feels like I’ve kind of blown the whole day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking a lot about your weight or body shape, or feel that they are very important to how you see yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m obsessed or anything, but before a meeting, or like if I’m going on a date, I definitely check the mirror. Mostly my stomach and my face. If my face looks kind of puffy or my shirt pulls weird, I start thinking, great, now I look soft.\n\nAnd in sales, people size you up fast—like, before you even say anything. I had this old manager who used to say your appearance walks into the room before you do, which, I don’t know, stuck with me. So if I think I look sloppy, I feel like people are going to see me as weak or not put together. So yeah, I guess it matters more than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done anything on purpose to try to keep from gaining weight, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m doing anything extreme like that. The throwing up thing, no, that’s not really… I mean, I hate throwing up, even when I had food poisoning once after this client dinner—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI do try to stay on top of things, like not let a meal get out of hand. And exercise… I mean, I’ll walk more if I feel like I’ve been sitting in the car all day, but that’s just normal, right? Sales is weird because you’re either sitting for hours or you’re pacing around a showroom pretending you’re not checking your phone every two seconds.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’m doing “weight loss tricks” or whatever. I just try not to overdo it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever skip meals, fast, or avoid eating certain types of food because you're worried about gaining weight or because you're trying to stay in control of your eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I do skip meals, I guess, but it doesn’t feel like “fasting” exactly. Most days it’s coffee in the morning, then if I’m between sales calls maybe I’ll have a protein bar, just something quick. Lunch is hard anyway because I’m usually driving or meeting someone, and honestly those client lunches can turn into this whole thing where everyone orders fries and appetizers and then you’re stuck sitting there pretending you’re relaxed.\n\nThen at night I’ll keep it small, like grilled chicken, no rice or anything. It’s not like I’m making some big plan, it’s just… if I start eating a full meal, it feels like I messed up the whole day, like I lost control of it. So I try to keep it simple.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself in the mirror, do you see yourself differently than others might describe you, or do you feel like certain parts of your body are bigger than they really are?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, kind of. Like before a meeting or if I’m getting ready for a date, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture, I guess. I go straight to my stomach, or my face.\n\nIf my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird, then I’m like, okay, I look soft. And then it turns into this whole thing in my head, like people are going to read that as sloppy or weak or like I don’t have my life together. Which is stupid, maybe, because most people are probably just thinking about themselves, but in sales you get used to watching every little reaction from people, you know? So it’s hard not to think they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever push yourself to exercise more, eat much less, or make any special rules around eating if you feel like you've eaten too much or had something 'unhealthy'?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like a written rulebook or anything, but if I have, like, a client dinner where everyone’s ordering drinks and sides and dessert—especially if I’m in Miami for the weekend or something, because there’s always some big meal—I’ll check the scale after and if it’s even up a pound, I kind of panic.\n\nIt’s not rational, I know a pound can be, like, water or salt or whatever, but in the moment it feels like, okay, I’m losing control, I let it go too far. Then I start thinking about the next day, like what I can cut back on, how to keep it tighter. I’ll be planning it in my head while I’m supposed to be doing normal stuff, like answering emails or driving to a meeting. So… yeah, I guess I do make up for it, or try to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself exercise more or eat a lot less the day after you’ve had something you consider unhealthy, like a dessert or a heavier meal?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much what happens. Like if I have a client dinner and it’s heavier than I wanted, or if I’m out in Miami for a weekend and there’s just food everywhere, I’ll check the scale after. And if it’s even up, like, one pound, I get this… panic feeling, like I screwed up or I’m losing control.\n\nThen I start planning the next day in my head—what I can cut back, how to keep it smaller. Sometimes I’m not even paying attention to other things because I’m already trying to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your overall sense of how you’re doing in life, or how you feel about yourself, is strongly connected to your weight or how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t love admitting that, but it does feel tied together.\n\nLike before a meeting or a date, I’ll check the mirror and I’m not really thinking, “Do I look fine overall?” I go straight to my stomach or my face. And if my face looks puffy or my shirt makes my stomach look… soft, then it’s like my whole confidence drops. I start thinking people are going to see me as sloppy, or weak, or like I’m not on top of things.\n\nWhich is probably dramatic, I know. I mean, I sell to people who are worrying about pricing and delivery dates, not my face, but still—once I see it, it kind of colors everything.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your weight or body shape has an undue influence on how you judge yourself overall?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess “undue” sounds like a lot, but yeah, it probably does.\n\nLike before I walk into a meeting, or even before a date, I’ll check the mirror and I don’t really see the whole picture. I go straight to my stomach or my face. If I think I look soft or puffy or whatever, it’s like I immediately feel less… capable, I guess. Like people are going to look at me and think I’m sloppy or weak, even if that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd I know most people aren’t thinking about that—like they’re probably thinking about their own stuff—but once I notice it, it kind of takes over how I feel about myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself and feel like you look soft or puffy, have others ever told you that you look different than you perceive yourself to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Like, someone might say, “You look fine,” or not notice anything at all, and I’m still stuck on it.\n\nIt’s usually before a meeting or a date—I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see the whole me, I just zero in on my stomach or my face. If my face looks puffy or my shirt sits weird and I think my stomach looks soft, then I start assuming that’s what everyone else is going to see too. Like they’ll think I’m sloppy or weak or not put together.\n\nAnd I know that’s probably not what they’re thinking. I mean, half the time people are worried about their own presentation or traffic or whatever. But in the moment it feels really obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating, like you can't stop eating once you start, and then do something to try to make up for it, like eating a lot less or exercising more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it like I can’t stop once I start. I mean… sometimes I eat more than I planned, like anyone does, especially if I’m stressed or there’s food around at work—people bring in donuts or whatever, and then everyone’s standing around talking about numbers and orders and I’m thinking about whether I should’ve had one.\n\nBut the “lost control” thing… I don’t know. That sounds a little different than what I usually think of. I do get in my head about it afterward, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by making up for it.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt like you couldn’t stop eating or control what or how much you were eating, even if you didn’t want to eat that much?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, “couldn’t stop” feels… strong. There are times I eat more than I meant to, like if I’m at work and someone brings in food and I’m distracted talking to people, and then afterward I’m like, why did I do that?\n\nBut I don’t know if it’s that I couldn’t control it, exactly. Sometimes it’s more like I’m thinking about it so much that the whole thing gets weird. Like I’ll have one thing and then spend the next hour replaying it, which is probably not what you’re asking.
